Thus, it becomes evident that the alignment of the cellular context in the pre-training phase plays a critical role in achieving superior results for downstream data integration tasks, even when datasets of similar sizes are compared. In light of our findings, the whole-human pre-trained model, embodying a vast spectrum of cell types, consistently demonstrates robust performance across diverse analyses.</p><p id="p-111">Our investigation underlined the significance of cellular context in single-cell RNA-seq data integration tasks. In certain circumstances, when the cellular context of the target dataset aligns with the tissue-specific pre-trained models, these models can excel. Overall, while it is essential to consider the cellular context, the whole-human pre-trained model emerges as a versatile and reliable option for a wide range of applications.</p></div><div class="section" id="sec-63"><h3>S.2 Benchmarking results on downstream tasks</h3><div id="T1" class="table pos-float"><div class="table-inline table-callout-links"><div class="callout"><span>View this table:</span><ul class="callout-links"><li class="view-inline first"><a href="" class="table-expand-inline" data-table-url="/highwire/markup/3237811/expansion?postprocessors=highwire_tables%2Chighwire_reclass%2Chighwire_figures%2Chighwire_math%2Chighwire_inline_linked_media%2Chighwire_embed&amp;table-expand-inline=1" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">View inline</a></li><li class="view-popup"><a href="/highwire/markup/3237811/expansion?width=1000&amp;height=500&amp;iframe=true&amp;postprocessors=highwire_tables%2Chighwire_reclass%2Chighwire_figures%2Chighwire_math%2Chighwire_inline_linked_media%2Chighwire_embed" class="colorbox colorbox-load table-expand-popup" rel="gallery-fragment-tables" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">View popup</a></li><li class="download-ppt last"><a href="/highwire/powerpoint/3237811" class="highwire-figure-link highwire-figure-link-ppt" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">Download powerpoint</a></li></ul></div></div><div class="table-caption"><span class="table-label">Table S1:</span> <p id="p-112" class="first-child">Cell Type Annotation Benchmark Results. scGPT was benchmarked with TOSICA [<a id="xref-ref-34-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-34">34</a>] and scBert [<a id="xref-ref-35-3" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-35">35</a>] on the Myeloid (Mye.), Multiple Sclerosis (M.S.), and hPancreas [<a id="xref-ref-34-5" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-34">34</a>] datasets for cell type annotation performance. We present four classification evaluation metrics <em>Accuracy, Precision, Recall</em>, and <em>MacroF</em> 1. See metric details in Supplementary Online Methods S.5.</p><div class="sb-div caption-clear"></div></div></div><div id="T2" class="table pos-float"><div class="table-inline table-callout-links"><div class="callout"><span>View this table:</span><ul class="callout-links"><li class="view-inline first"><a href="" class="table-expand-inline" data-table-url="/highwire/markup/3237804/expansion?postprocessors=highwire_tables%2Chighwire_reclass%2Chighwire_figures%2Chighwire_math%2Chighwire_inline_linked_media%2Chighwire_embed&amp;table-expand-inline=1" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">View inline</a></li><li class="view-popup"><a href="/highwire/markup/3237804/expansion?width=1000&amp;height=500&amp;iframe=true&amp;postprocessors=highwire_tables%2Chighwire_reclass%2Chighwire_figures%2Chighwire_math%2Chighwire_inline_linked_media%2Chighwire_embed" class="colorbox colorbox-load table-expand-popup" rel="gallery-fragment-tables" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">View popup</a></li><li class="download-ppt last"><a href="/highwire/powerpoint/3237804" class="highwire-figure-link highwire-figure-link-ppt" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">Download powerpoint</a></li></ul></div></div><div class="table-caption"><span class="table-label">Table S2:</span> <p id="p-113" class="first-child">scRNA-seq Integration Benchmark Results. scGPT was benchmarked with scVI [<a id="xref-ref-41-3" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-41">41</a>], Seurat [<a id="xref-ref-42-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-42">42</a>], and Harmony [<a id="xref-ref-43-3" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-43">43</a>] on the COVID-19 (18 batches) [<a id="xref-ref-13-6" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-13">13</a>], PBMC 10K (2 batches) [<a id="xref-ref-44-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-44">44</a>] and Perirhinal Cortex (2 batches) [<a id="xref-ref-45-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-45">45</a>] datasets for cell type clustering and batch correction performance. We present three aggregate scores <em>AvgBIO, AvgBATCH</em>, and <em>Overall</em>. These aggregate scores were calculated from three detailed biological conservation metrics (<em>NMI</em><sub><em>cell</em></sub>, <em>ARI</em><sub><em>cell</em></sub>, <em>ASW</em><sub><em>cell</em></sub>) and two batch correction metrics (<em>ASW</em><sub><em>batch</em></sub>, <em>GraphConn</em>). See metric details in Supplementary Online Methods S.5</p><div class="sb-div caption-clear"></div></div></div><div id="T3" class="table pos-float"><div class="table-inline table-callout-links"><div class="callout"><span>View this table:</span><ul class="callout-links"><li class="view-inline first"><a href="" class="table-expand-inline" data-table-url="/highwire/markup/3237817/expansion?postprocessors=highwire_tables%2Chighwire_reclass%2Chighwire_figures%2Chighwire_math%2Chighwire_inline_linked_media%2Chighwire_embed&amp;table-expand-inline=1" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">View inline</a></li><li class="view-popup"><a href="/highwire/markup/3237817/expansion?width=1000&amp;height=500&amp;iframe=true&amp;postprocessors=highwire_tables%2Chighwire_reclass%2Chighwire_figures%2Chighwire_math%2Chighwire_inline_linked_media%2Chighwire_embed" class="colorbox colorbox-load table-expand-popup" rel="gallery-fragment-tables" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">View popup</a></li><li class="download-ppt last"><a href="/highwire/powerpoint/3237817" class="highwire-figure-link highwire-figure-link-ppt" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">Download powerpoint</a></li></ul></div></div><div class="table-caption"><span class="table-label">Table S3:</span> <p id="p-114" class="first-child">scMultiomic Integration Benchmark Results. For the paired 10X Multiome PBMC[<a id="xref-ref-47-5" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-47">47</a>] dataset, scGPT was benchmarked with scGLUE[<a id="xref-ref-14-7" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-14">14</a>] and Seurat v4[<a id="xref-ref-46-6" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-46">46</a>] for cell type clustering performance evaluated on four biological conservation metrics. The data has only one technical batch. Batch correction metrics are not applicable to this setting. For the paired BMMC[<a id="xref-ref-48-5" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-48">48</a>] and mosaic ASAP PBMC[<a id="xref-ref-49-6" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-49">49</a>] datasets, scGPT was benchmarked with Seurat v4 and scMoMat [<a id="xref-ref-15-6" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-15">15</a>] respectively on cell type clustering and multi-omic integration performance, evaluated on eight biological conservation and batch correction metrics.</p><div class="sb-div caption-clear"></div></div></div><div id="T4" class="table pos-float"><div class="table-inline table-callout-links"><div class="callout"><span>View this table:</span><ul class="callout-links"><li class="view-inline first"><a href="" class="table-expand-inline" data-table-url="/highwire/markup/3237808/expansion?postprocessors=highwire_tables%2Chighwire_reclass%2Chighwire_figures%2Chighwire_math%2Chighwire_inline_linked_media%2Chighwire_embed&amp;table-expand-inline=1" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">View inline</a></li><li class="view-popup"><a href="/highwire/markup/3237808/expansion?width=1000&amp;height=500&amp;iframe=true&amp;postprocessors=highwire_tables%2Chighwire_reclass%2Chighwire_figures%2Chighwire_math%2Chighwire_inline_linked_media%2Chighwire_embed" class="colorbox colorbox-load table-expand-popup" rel="gallery-fragment-tables" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">View popup</a></li><li class="download-ppt last"><a href="/highwire/powerpoint/3237808" class="highwire-figure-link highwire-figure-link-ppt" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">Download powerpoint</a></li></ul></div></div><div class="table-caption"><span class="table-label">Table S4:</span> <p id="p-115" class="first-child">Comparison of Common and Unique Pathways Identified by scGPT and the Coexpression Network From the Reactome Database. The enriched pathways from gene programs extracted by both methods at Leiden resolution 40 are listed here for comparison, corresponding to the Venn diagram in <a id="xref-fig-5-7" class="xref-fig" href="#F5">Figure 5 E</a>. The 14 immune-related pathways uniquely identified by scGPT are highlighted in bold.</p><div class="sb-div caption-clear"></div></div></div></div><div class="section" id="sec-64"><h3>S.3 Enhancing Speed and Decreasing Memory Usage in Fine-tunings</h3><p id="p-116">In an attempt to hasten the fine-tuning phase and render scGPT more accessible for users, we explored a variety of fine-tuning methods in the context of the scRNA-seq integration task. The pre-trained weights from the whole-human dataset were employed to initialize our entire model. As the standard practice, the baseline Full fine-tune procedure involves gradient updates for all model parameters and includes all zero-expressed genes. We undertook experiments that involved freezing the embedding layers and omitting zero-expressed genes during fine-tuning. From data presented in table S5, we noticed a significant drop in the training time per epoch as well as in GPU Memory usage throughout the fine-tuning process. Specifically, the removal of zero-expressed genes resulted in a substantial reduction in our model’s maximum sequence length to approximately 40 <em>∼</em> 60% of its initial length, effectively halving both the time spent on training epochs and peak GPU Memory utilization. Furthermore, by freezing the embedding layer, we achieved an additional reduction in Peak GPU Memory usage by 1GB, a slight increase in training speed, while preserving a comparable AvgBio score.</p><div id="T5" class="table pos-float"><div class="table-inline table-callout-links"><div class="callout"><span>View this table:</span><ul class="callout-links"><li class="view-inline first"><a href="" class="table-expand-inline" data-table-url="/highwire/markup/3237813/expansion?postprocessors=highwire_tables%2Chighwire_reclass%2Chighwire_figures%2Chighwire_math%2Chighwire_inline_linked_media%2Chighwire_embed&amp;table-expand-inline=1" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">View inline</a></li><li class="view-popup"><a href="/highwire/markup/3237813/expansion?width=1000&amp;height=500&amp;iframe=true&amp;postprocessors=highwire_tables%2Chighwire_reclass%2Chighwire_figures%2Chighwire_math%2Chighwire_inline_linked_media%2Chighwire_embed" class="colorbox colorbox-load table-expand-popup" rel="gallery-fragment-tables" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">View popup</a></li><li class="download-ppt last"><a href="/highwire/powerpoint/3237813" class="highwire-figure-link highwire-figure-link-ppt" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">Download powerpoint</a></li></ul></div></div><div class="table-caption"><span class="table-label">Table S5:</span> <p id="p-117" class="first-child">Benchmarking Results for scRNA-seq Integration Speed Enhancement Techniques. scGPT was evaluated based on different fine-tuning strategies on four datasets: COVID-19 (18 batches) [<a id="xref-ref-13-7" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-13">13</a>], PBMC 10K (2 batches) [<a id="xref-ref-44-5" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-44">44</a>], and Perirhinal Cortex (2 batches) [<a id="xref-ref-45-5" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-45">45</a>]. These evaluations were performed to assess cell type clustering and batch correction performance. This table encapsulates two system metrics: the average duration of training per epoch and the maximum GPU memory usage on an A100 GPU. The resulting <em>AvgBio</em> score is also presented for performance comparisons.</p><div class="sb-div caption-clear"></div></div></div></div><div class="section" id="sec-65"><h3>S.4 Comparison to existing approaches</h3><div class="section" id="sec-66"><h4>Transformers for modelling single-cell sequencing data</h4><p id="p-118">Transformer models employing self-attention mechanisms [<a id="xref-ref-27-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-27">27</a>] have demonstrated remarkable success in the field of natural language processing (NLP) [<a id="xref-ref-64-3" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-64">64</a>], computer vision [<a id="xref-ref-87-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-87">87</a>], and protein folding prediction [<a id="xref-ref-88-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-88">88</a>]. However, there have been few efforts to incorporate the transformer architecture into single-cell biology and its related applications. Shen et al. [<a id="xref-ref-89-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-89">89</a>] utilize a transformer decoder setup to learn the sequence of the names of highly expressed genes, but they do not take into account the actual sequenced expression abundance, resulting in the loss of crucial biological information. scBERT [<a id="xref-ref-35-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-35">35</a>] and TOSICA [<a id="xref-ref-34-6" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-34">34</a>] used BERT-like architectures [<a id="xref-ref-64-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-64">64</a>] to train cell embeddings but only applied the model on the supervised task of cell annotation. Similarly, Connell, Khan, and Keiser [<a id="xref-ref-90-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-90">90</a>] utilized transformer encoders mainly to predict genetic perturbation responses. To our best knowledge, scGPT is one of the first methods to provide a generative pre-trained transformer foundation model for multiple single-cell analysis tasks.</p></div><div class="section" id="sec-67"><h4>Pre-training on large cell atlases</h4><p id="p-119">Although the idea of employing pre-training and finetuning on a wide range of downstream tasks as a unified framework remains largely unexplored, several works have attempted to use transfer learning on specific tasks. scArches [<a id="xref-ref-13-8" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-13">13</a>] devised a transfer-learning-based approach for reference mapping by pre-training a conditional variational autoencoder (VAE) on the reference datasets. However, the scale of the reference datasets remains limited, and the VAE-based architecture does not incorporate attention computation. On the other hand, scBERT [<a id="xref-ref-35-5" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-35">35</a>] is pre-trained on 1 million cells with a BERT architecture. However, the downstream application focuses on cell type annotation only, thus restricting the generalizability of the pre-training and fine-tuning strategy. A recent work, Geneformer [<a id="xref-ref-91-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-91">91</a>], has extended pretrained transformers beyond cell type annotation to gene network analysis, but the work didn’t demonstrate abilities for perturbation response prediction or multi-omic integration. scGPT has compiled an unprecedented scale of pre-training data and evaluated on a diverse range of downstream tasks, which presents the pre-training and fine-tuning strategy as a unified framework for versatile single-cell analysis.</p></div><div class="section" id="sec-68"><h4>Learning cell and gene representation for scRNA-seq on downstream tasks</h4><p id="p-120">Cell representation learning facilitates a variety of downstream tasks such as cell type annotation, multi-omic integration, and perturbation prediction. A popular framework Seurat [<a id="xref-ref-42-5" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-42">42</a>, <a id="xref-ref-92-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-92">92</a>] employs nearest-neighbor-based alignment to remove batch effect via linear transformation in the embedding space. LIGER [<a id="xref-ref-93-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-93">93</a>] and OCAT [<a id="xref-ref-94-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-94">94</a>] use matrix factorization to extract latent cell embeddings. Recently, Deep Learning methods and especially VAE-based generative models have gained increasing popularity, as they generate deep embeddings via non-linear transformations through neural networks [<a id="xref-ref-95-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-95">95</a>]. scVI [<a id="xref-ref-41-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-41">41</a>] learns latent cell representations by reconstructing original gene expression via variational inference. TotalVI [<a id="xref-ref-96-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-96">96</a>], scGen [<a id="xref-ref-11-2" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-11">11</a>], CPA [<a id="xref-ref-12-3" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-12">12</a>] and MultiCPA [<a id="xref-ref-97-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-97">97</a>] utilized similar models and extended the application to multi-omics and perturbation prediction. On the other hand, gene representation learning also supports many downstream tasks including gene regulatory network and functional pathway analysis. As an example, GeneVector [<a id="xref-ref-77-2" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-77">77</a>] detects gene-gene functional relations by factorizing the co-expression and mutual information matrix of the sequencing readout. VEGA [<a id="xref-ref-98-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-98">98</a>] utilizes a sparse VAE architecture to encode gene network activities for added interpretability. DeepMAPS [<a id="xref-ref-99-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-99">99</a>] utilizes graph neural networks to encode cell and gene nodes for related tasks. Despite the importance of the two branches of research in cell and gene embedding learning, few approaches have worked on jointly learning both. scGPT stands out as an approach to effectively learn both embeddings of cells and genes jointly in a shared architecture.</p></div></div><div class="section" id="sec-69"><h3>S.5 Evaluation Metric Calculations</h3><div class="section" id="sec-70"><h4>S.5.1 Cell Type Assignment</h4><p id="p-121">We used the standard classification metrics <em>Accuracy, Precision, Recall</em>, and <em>MacroF</em> 1 to evaluate cell type assignment performance. The <em>Accuracy, Precision, Recall</em>, and <em>MacroF</em> 1 scores are calculated from true positives (<em>tp</em>), false positives (<em>fp</em>), true negatives (<em>tn</em>), and false negatives (<em>fn</em>) globally or averaged per class.</p><p id="p-122">The <em>Accuracy, Precision</em> and <em>Recall</em> scores are calculated globally:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-14"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-25.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-25.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
The <em>MacroF</em> 1 score is calculated per cell type <em>c</em> first and averaged across cell types:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-15"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-26.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-26.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
The above metrics are calculated using scikit-learn’s implementations [<a id="xref-ref-100-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-100">100</a>].</p></div><div class="section" id="sec-71"><h4>S.5.2 Reverse Perturbation - Predicting driving gene perturbations with TopK Retrieval</h4><p id="p-123">We employed two modified topK retrieval accuracy metrics to assess the reverse perturbation prediction performance. The hit rate of correct predictions calculates the proportion of test cases where the topK retrieved experiments contain the target (i.e., query) condition. For example, for each test case of target condition X+Y, if the topK retrieved experiments contain X+Y, we count this test case as a hit. We also reported a relaxed topK accuracy metric for relevant retrievals with one-gene overlap. The percentage of test cases with relevant predictions calculates the proportion of test cases where the topK retrieved experiments contain any cases with a one-gene overlap with the target condition. For example, for the same test case X+Y, if the topK retrieved experiments contain X, Y, X+A, A+X, Y+A, or A+Y, we count this test case as a hit. This relaxed metric aims to provide added interpretability for scGPT’s choices of retrieval.</p></div><div class="section" id="sec-72"><h4>S.5.3 Single-cell integration</h4><p id="p-124">We adopted the evaluation metric calculations outlined by Luecken et al. [<a id="xref-ref-53-8" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-53">53</a>] in their benchmark study. Each metric is described below.</p><div class="section" id="sec-73"><h5>Normalized Mutual Information</h5><p id="p-125">To quantify the concurrence between the cell type labels based on ground truth and the Louvain cluster labels obtained from integrated cell embeddings, we computed the normalized mutual information (NMI) score. The Louvain clustering was conducted across resolutions ranging from 0.1 to 2, with increments of 0.1. The best score will be selected. The NMI score for cell types, referred to as <strong><em>NMI</em><sub><em>cell</em></sub></strong>, ranges between 0 and 1, where a higher score indicates a better match of cell types.</p></div><div class="section" id="sec-74"><h5>Adjusted Rand Index</h5><p id="p-126">The adjusted rand index (ARI) was employed to assess both the agreement between the annotated labels and the MNI-optimized Louvain clusters. Furthermore, the rand index was adjusted to account for randomly correct labels. The ARI score for cell types, denoted as <strong><em>ARI</em><sub><em>cell</em></sub></strong>, ranges from 0 to 1, where 0 corresponds to random labeling and 1 represents a perfect match.</p></div><div class="section" id="sec-75"><h5>Average Silhouette Width</h5><p id="p-127">The silhouette width assesses the relationship between a cell’s within-cluster distances and its distances to the closest cluster boundaries. By averaging the silhouette widths of all cells, we calculate the average silhouette width (ASW) score. This score ranges from -1 to 1, where a score of 1 indicates well-separated clusters, while scores from -1 to 0 suggest overlapping clusters and misclassification.</p><p id="p-128">For evaluating cell type clustering, we compute the ASW score based on cell type labels, represented as <strong><em>ASW</em><sub><em>cell</em></sub></strong>. To obtain this score, we utilize the following formula:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-16"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-27.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-27.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
Here, <em>C</em> represents the cell types.</p><p id="p-129">Regarding batch mixing evaluation, we calculate the ASW score considering batch labels and adjust it by subtracting 1. This score is denoted as <strong><em>ASW</em><sub><em>batch</em></sub></strong>. The calculation is as follows:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-17"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-28.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-28.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
Both <strong><em>ASW</em><sub><em>cell</em></sub></strong> and <strong><em>ASW</em><sub><em>batch</em></sub></strong> have values between 0 and 1. Higher scores indicate better cell-type clustering or batch-mixing performance.</p></div><div class="section" id="sec-76"><h5>Graph Connectivity</h5><p id="p-130">The graph connectivity metric quantifies the average proportion of cells within each cell type that are connected through a kNN (k-nearest neighbors) graph. For every cell identity <em>c</em> in the set <em>C</em>, we compute the size of the largest connected component using kNN among cells exclusively belonging to identity <em>c</em>. This value is divided by the total number of cells with identity <em>c</em> to obtain a normalized measure. The <strong><em>GraphConn</em></strong> score is then reported as the average across all cell types:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-18"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-29.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-29.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
Here, <em>LCC</em> represents the largest connected component, and <em>N</em> denotes the number of cells of each celltype.</p></div><div class="section" id="sec-77"><h5>Aggregated Metrics</h5><p id="p-131">The aggregated metric <strong><em>AvgBIO</em></strong> calculates the average of biological conservation metrics:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-19"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-30.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-30.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
Similarly, the aggregated metric <strong><em>AvgBAT CH</em></strong> computes the average of batch mixing metrics:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-20"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-31.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-31.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
In accordance with the convention established in [<a id="xref-ref-53-9" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-53">53</a>], an <strong><em>Overall</em></strong> metric is derived as the weighted average of <strong><em>AvgBIO</em></strong> and <strong><em>AvgBAT CH</em></strong>:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-21"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-32.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/embed/graphic-32.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p></div></div></div></div><div class="section" id="sec-78"><h2 class="">Supplementary Figures</h2><div id="F7" class="fig pos-float type-figure  odd"><div class="highwire-figure"><div class="fig-inline-img-wrapper"><div class="fig-inline-img"><a href="https://www.biorxiv.org/content/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/F7.large.jpg?width=800&amp;height=600&amp;carousel=1" title="The scGPT Attention Mask. The token identity associated with each column is annotated below, namely &#x201C;cell emb &lt; cls &gt;&#x201C;for cell embedding, &#x201C;genes &amp; expression&#x201D; for known genes, and &#x201C;genes to predict&#x201D; for unknown genes. (A) scGPT attention mask in training where only query gene and the known genes participate in attention computation. (B) After training, the attention mask at each step during the iterative process of scGPT cell-prompt generation." src="https://www.biorxiv.org/content/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/F7.medium.gif" width="440" height="137"/></noscript></span></a></div></div><ul class="highwire-figure-links inline"><li class="download-fig first"><a href="https://www.biorxiv.org/content/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/F7.large.jpg?download=true" class="highwire-figure-link highwire-figure-link-download" title="Download Figure S1:" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">Download figure</a></li><li class="new-tab last"><a href="https://www.biorxiv.org/content/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/F7.large.jpg" class="highwire-figure-link highwire-figure-link-newtab" target="_blank" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">Open in new tab</a></li></ul></div><div class="fig-caption"><span class="fig-label">Figure S1:</span> <p id="p-132" class="first-child">The scGPT Attention Mask. The masked positions are colored in blue, and the unmasked positions in white. These masked and unmasked positions correspond to the <em>M × M</em> attention map for <em>M</em> input tokens. The row indices correspond to queries and columns correspond to keys. In the self-attention computation of transformers, the attention scores on the masked positions will be removed. The token identity associated with each column is annotated below, namely “cell emb <em>&lt; cls &gt;</em>“for cell embedding, “genes &amp; expression” for known genes, and “genes to predict” for unknown genes. <em>(A)</em> scGPT attention mask in training where only query gene and the known genes participate in attention computation. <em>(B)</em> After training, the attention mask at each step during the iterative process of scGPT cell-prompt generation.</p><div class="sb-div caption-clear"></div></div></div><div id="F8" class="fig pos-float type-figure  odd"><div class="highwire-figure"><div class="fig-inline-img-wrapper"><div class="fig-inline-img"><a href="https://www.biorxiv.org/content/biorxiv/early/2023/07/02/2023.04.30.538439/F8.large.jpg?width=800&amp;height=600&amp;carousel=1" title="UMAP of 3 million cancer cells using the cell embeddings from the pre-trained pancancer model. The COVID-19 dataset comprises a range of samples, including those derived from COVID-19, lung, bone marrow, and PBMC data, reflecting its cellular context. (A) Illustration of the average AvgBIO score along with the standard error derived from five scRNA-seq integration experiments for each pre-training model. The AvgBIO score provides a quantitative measure of the efficacy of each pre-trained model in integrating batches. The models are ordered by their average score, highlighting the potential of tissue-specific pre-training in improving the performance of integration. In-context models (Blood and Lung), which align with the cell types in the target dataset, are separated from out-of-context models (Heart, Brain, Kidney, Pancreas, Intestine) by annotation brackets, highlighting the influence of cellular context on the integration performance. (B) UMAP visualization of the cell embeddings, colored by cell types, achieved by fine-tuning each of the tissue-specific pre-training models. The COVID-19 dataset comprises a range of samples, including those derived from COVID-19, lung, bone marrow, and PBMC data, reflecting its cellular context. <em>(A)</em> Illustration of the average <em>AvgBIO</em> score along with the standard error derived from five scRNA-seq integration experiments for each pre-training model. The <em>AvgBIO</em> score provides a quantitative measure of the efficacy of each pre-trained model in integrating batches. The models are ordered by their average score, highlighting the potential of tissue-specific pre-training in improving the performance of integration. In-context models (Blood and Lung), which align with the cell types in the target dataset, are separated from out-of-context models (Heart, Brain, Kidney, Pancreas, Intestine) by annotation brackets, highlighting the influence of cellular context on the integration performance. <em>(B)</em> UMAP visualization of the cell embeddings, colored by <em>cell types</em>, achieved by fine-tuning each of the tissue-specific pre-training models. The plots depict the quality of learned representations in the context of cell type diversity, offering visual confirmation of the models’ integration capabilities.</p><div class="sb-div caption-clear"></div></div></div></div><div class="section ack" id="ack-1"><h2 class="">Acknowledgement</h2><p id="p-107">We would like to express our sincere gratitude to, Dr. Nan Duan, for his invaluable guidance and support throughout the project.
